\title{A Textbook about the Paper: "xLSTM: Extended Long Short-Term Memory"}

# Chapter 1: The Evolution of Sequence Modeling and the Revival of LSTMs

## Historical Overview: From Constant Error Carousel to Transformer Dominance

The story of sequence modeling in deep learning began in earnest during the 1990s with the introduction of the Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) network. Before this innovation, standard Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) struggled significantly with the vanishing gradient problem, a phenomenon where gradients diminish exponentially as they are backpropagated through time, rendering it nearly impossible for networks to learn dependencies spanning long temporal distances. To overcome this fundamental barrier, researchers introduced two central ideas: the constant error carousel and gating mechanisms. The constant error carousel refers to an additive update mechanism within a dedicated memory cell that allows error signals to flow backward through time without decaying, effectively creating a path for gradients to persist over long sequences. This concept was paired with gating units—specifically input, forget, and output gates—that regulate the flow of information into, out of, and within this memory cell. These innovations allowed LSTMs to stand the test of time for decades, becoming the backbone of numerous deep learning success stories. They were instrumental in pioneering early Large Language Models (LLMs) and excelled in diverse domains ranging from text generation and handwriting synthesis to machine translation and even complex reinforcement learning tasks like controlling nuclear fusion reactors or playing professional-level games such as StarCraft II and Dota 2.

However, despite their robustness and proven efficacy in learning abstractions like syntax and semantics, the landscape of artificial intelligence shifted dramatically with the advent of the Transformer architecture in 2017. The Transformer introduced self-attention mechanisms that were inherently parallelizable, allowing for massive scalability that sequential recurrent models could not match at the time. While LSTMs continued to serve niche applications requiring extreme efficiency or specific types of abstraction extraction, they were largely superseded by Transformers in large-scale language modeling. The inability of LSTMs to leverage modern hardware efficiently due to their sequential nature meant they fell behind as model sizes grew into the billions of parameters. Yet, this displacement raises a compelling question regarding the theoretical limits of recurrent architectures: If we take the foundational principles of LSTMs but systematically mitigate their known structural limitations using modern techniques derived from current Large Language Models, how far can we push their performance? This inquiry forms the basis for revisiting and extending LSTM capabilities to compete once again with state-of-the-art attention-based models.

## Mathematical Foundations of the Original LSTM (Cell State, Input/Forget/Output Gates)

To understand how xLSTM extends these concepts, one must first rigorously define the mechanics of the original LSTM. At its core lies a scalar memory cell state, denoted as $c_t$, which acts as a conveyor belt for information across time steps. The evolution of this cell state is governed by an additive update rule designed to preserve information flow while selectively modifying content based on incoming data. Mathematically, this update is expressed as $c_t = f_t c_{t-1} + i_t z_t$. Here, $c_{t-1}$ represents the previous cell state carried forward from time step $t-1$. The term $f_t$ is the forget gate activation value, which modulates how much of the previous state is retained; if $f_t$ is close to zero, old information is discarded, whereas a value near one preserves it entirely. Simultaneously, new candidate information $z_t$ is generated from the current input $\mathbf{x}_t$ and potentially filtered by an input gate $i_t$. This new information is added to the scaled previous state to form the updated cell state $c_t$.

Once the cell state is updated, it does not directly become the output; instead, it passes through an output gate mechanism to produce the hidden state $h_t$, which serves as both the output for that time step and part of the input for subsequent calculations. This relationship is defined by $h_t = o_t \psi(c_t)$, where $\psi$ is a non-linear activation function (typically $\tanh$) used to normalize or squash the unbounded cell state values into a manageable range. The output gate $o_t$ controls how much of this normalized internal representation is exposed externally. Crucially, all three gates—the input gate $i_t$, forget gate $f_t$, and output gate $o_t$—are computed using sigmoid activation functions $\sigma(x) = 1/(1+\exp(-x))$, ensuring their values lie strictly between 0 and 1. Each gate receives contributions from both external inputs $\mathbf{x}_t$ via weight vectors $\mathbf{w}$ and recurrent connections from the previous hidden state $h_{t-1}$ via recurrent weights $r$. For instance, pre-gate activations like $\tilde{f}_t = \mathbf{w}^\top_f \mathbf{x}_t + r_f h_{t-1} + b_f$ are calculated before passing through the sigmoid function. This intricate system of multiplicative gating allows LSTMs to dynamically manage memory retention and access but relies heavily on scalar representations that impose specific constraints on capacity and flexibility.

## Limitation I: Inability to Revise Storage Decisions (The Nearest Neighbor Search Problem)

A primary structural weakness inherent in traditional LSTMs is their inability to easily revise storage decisions once made during sequential processing. Because standard LSTMs rely on sigmoid gates bounded between 0 and 1 for forgetting ($f_t$), once a piece of information has been partially forgotten or overwritten by subsequent updates involving smaller gate values, recovering or correcting that decision becomes mathematically difficult without significant interference from new data streams that may not align perfectly with prior errors. This limitation manifests starkly in tasks requiring dynamic updating based on global context comparisons within a sequence. A canonical example illustrating this failure mode is the Nearest Neighbor Search problem. In this scenario, given a reference vector at some point in time or implicitly held in working memory, an agent must scan a sequence sequentially to find another vector most similar to it and retrieve its associated value at the end of the sequence. As new vectors arrive that are increasingly similar to the reference vector than previously stored candidates should be selected over older ones—a process requiring continuous revision of what constitutes "best." Standard LSTMs struggle here because their gating mechanism tends to lock onto initial matches; when a superior match appears later in the sequence often fails sufficiently suppresses earlier entries The inability to revise storage decisions represents a fundamental constraint in standard LSTM architectures when faced with tasks requiring dynamic updates based on evolving contextual relevance. This issue arises because traditional LSTMs utilize sigmoid activation functions for their forget gates ($f_t = \sigma(\tilde{f}_t)$), which constrain gate values strictly between 0 and 1. Consequently, once information has been attenuated by a forget gate operation earlier in a sequence—effectively deciding that certain data should be discarded—it becomes mathematically challenging for subsequent operations to fully restore or prioritize that information if new evidence suggests it was actually critical later on. The multiplicative nature of these gates creates a "one-way street" effect where errors in early storage decisions compound rather than correct themselves naturally over time unless explicitly overridden by overwhelming new input signals that happen to align perfectly with past mistakes—an unlikely occurrence in complex scenarios requiring precise recall adjustments mid-stream processing ### Limitation II: Limited Storage Capacity for Rare Tokens (Scalar vs Matrix Memory)

Beyond issues related revisability another significant bottleneck facing conventional Long Short Term Memory networks stems directly from limited storage capacities inherent design choices specifically regarding scalar versus matrix representations within memory cells themselves traditionally implemented single valued scalars meaning entire history compressed down into one number essentially forcing extremely aggressive compression algorithms discard vast amounts nuanced detail contained raw inputs particularly problematic dealing rare events infrequent occurrences low Another critical limitation identified is **Limited Storage Capacity**, specifically concerning how standard LSTMs compress vast amounts of sequential information into scalar cell states ($c \in \mathbb{R}$). Because each timestep's contribution must be merged into this single dimension alongside previous accumulated knowledge through additive updates mediated by gating mechanisms there simply isn't sufficient room retain distinct details about every unique item encountered especially those occurring rarely throughout long sequences filled mostly common frequent elements dominating statistical # Chapter 2: Core Innovations I - Exponential Gating and Stabilization

## From Sigmoid to Exponential Gates: Theory and Benefits

The foundational architecture of the Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) network relies heavily on gating mechanisms to regulate the flow of information through time. In the classic formulation introduced in the 1990s, these gates—specifically the input gate $i_t$, forget gate $f_t$, and output gate $o_t$—utilize the sigmoid activation function $\sigma(x) = 1/(1+\exp(-x))$. The sigmoid function maps any real-valued input to a bounded interval between 0 and 1. While this boundedness ensures that the magnitude of updates remains controlled, it imposes a strict ceiling on how aggressively the network can modify its internal state. The primary innovation proposed in the xLSTM framework addresses this constraint by replacing the sigmoid activation with an exponential function for specific gates, particularly the input gate and optionally the forget gate. This shift from a saturating, bounded non-linearity to an unbounded exponential growth function fundamentally alters the dynamics of memory updating. By employing an exponential activation, defined as $\exp(\tilde{g}_t)$ where $\tilde{g}_t$ is a linear projection of inputs and previous hidden states, the model gains the theoretical capacity to generate arbitrarily large weighting factors. This capability is critical for scenarios requiring rapid adaptation or drastic revisions of stored information, allowing the network to effectively "overwrite" old memories with high-confidence new signals without being throttled by the asymptotic limit of unity inherent in sigmoidal gating.

## The Role of Exponential Gating in Revising Stored Information

One of the most significant limitations identified in traditional LSTMs is their inability to efficiently revise storage decisions once information has been committed to the cell state. This deficiency is starkly illustrated by tasks such as Nearest Neighbor Search, where a model must scan a sequence to find a vector most similar to a reference vector and retain only that associated value at the end of the sequence. In a standard LSTM utilizing sigmoid gates, if an initial candidate vector is deemed sufficiently similar and stored with a moderate weight, finding a subsequently superior candidate requires increasing its own weight while simultaneously decreasing the influence of the prior storage via the forget gate. Because both gates are constrained to values less than or equal to 1, this process often results in a slow, gradual attenuation rather than an immediate replacement. The network struggles to completely discard inferior information in favor of superior information discovered later in the sequence.

Exponential gating directly remediate this limitation by enabling dynamic range expansion in decision-making weights. When an exponentially activated input gate encounters a highly relevant new piece of information, it can produce a value significantly greater than 1. Simultaneously, if paired with an exponential forget gate or simply relying on relative magnitudes, this large input weight can dominate previous contributions instantly. Mathematically, if $i_t$ represents an exponentially scaled importance score for new data $z_t$, and $f_t$ represents retention probability for old data $c_{t-1}$, an extremely large $i_t$ allows the term $i_t z_t$ to overwhelm $f_t c_{t-1}$ regardless of how close $f_t$ is to 1. This mechanism empowers xLSTM units to perform true "revision," effectively swapping out outdated or incorrect associations with newly discovered correct ones immediately upon observation. This capability transforms the memory cell from a passive accumulator into an active repository capable of correcting its own historical records based on evolving evidence within a sequence.

## Designing the Normalizer State ($n_t$): Tracking Cumulative Gate Products

While exponential gating offers superior flexibility for revising memory, it introduces a fundamental instability regarding scale consistency if left unchecked. In standard LSTMs with sigmoid gates, since all weights are between 0 and 1, repeated multiplications naturally tend toward zero unless actively maintained near unity by biases; however, this bounded nature implicitly prevents runaway growth without explicit normalization logic beyond standard activations like tanh on hidden states. With exponential gates producing values potentially spanning several orders of magnitude above or below 1 depending on their raw logits $\tilde{i}_t$, simply adding these terms into a recursive equation like $c_t = f_t c_{t-1} + i_t z_t$ leads to unpredictable scaling behaviors where valid signal magnitudes become indistinguishable from noise due to arbitrary inflation or deflation over time steps. To resolve this, xLSTM introduces a dedicated auxiliary variable known as the normalizer state, denoted as $n_t$.

The normalizer state serves as a running tracker for the cumulative effect of all gating operations applied along temporal paths leading up to time step $t$. Specifically for sLSTM (scalar LSTM), which utilizes scalar memory cells with memory mixing across cells within heads but not necessarily across heads themselves initially described here conceptually before diving deeper into multi-cell specifics later chapters may cover more extensively though we stick strictly provided text scope now focusing single cell case primarily unless specified otherwise but actually looking at equations given:

# Chapter 3: Core Innovations II - The sLSTM Variant

The evolution of the Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) network into the Extended Long Short-Term Memory (xLSTM) architecture addresses fundamental limitations inherent in classical recurrent designs. While the previous chapter explored the matrix-based mLSTM designed for massive storage capacity and parallelization, this chapter focuses on the scalar variant, known as sLSTM. The primary motivation for developing sLSTM stems from a critical deficiency in traditional LSTMs: the inability to effectively revise storage decisions once information has been committed to the cell state. In tasks requiring dynamic updates, such as identifying the nearest neighbor in a stream of data or tracking complex logical states like parity, standard LSTMs often struggle to overwrite old information with newly discovered, more relevant data. The sLSTM resolves this by introducing exponential gating mechanisms coupled with rigorous normalization and stabilization techniques. Unlike its matrix counterpart, which sacrifices sequential dependencies for speed, the sLSTM retains and enhances the concept of memory mixing through hidden-to-gate recurrent connections. This design choice empowers the model to perform sophisticated state tracking, making it uniquely capable of solving formal language problems that stump both Transformers and simpler State Space Models. By integrating these innovations into a scalar memory framework, sLSTM bridges the gap between the historical robustness of LSTMs and the demands of modern large-scale language modeling.

# Architecture of the Scalar LSTM (sLSTM)

The architectural foundation of the scalar LSTM (sLSTM) builds directly upon the classic LSTM formulation but introduces profound modifications to its gating dynamics and normalization strategies. At its core, the sLSTM maintains a single scalar cell state $c_t$ at each time step $t$, serving as a compact repository for temporal information. However, unlike traditional LSTMs where gates are bounded by sigmoid activations that squash values into a fixed interval between zero and one, the sLSTM employs exponential activation functions for its input and forget gates. This shift is not merely cosmetic; it fundamentally alters how information flows into and persists within the memory cell. In standard LSTMs, if an input gate is slightly activated but a subsequent forget gate decides to retain old information strongly, updating that specific piece of information can be difficult because the multiplicative interaction limits flexibility. With exponential gating, the model gains a continuous, unbounded range to modulate these flows, theoretically allowing for more precise control over when to write new data versus when to preserve existing traces.

Crucially, replacing sigmoid functions with exponentials introduces significant numerical instability risks, as exponential growth can rapidly lead to overflow errors during computation. To mitigate this without sacrificing performance or altering the underlying mathematical properties of the network gradients, the sLSTM incorporates a dedicated stabilizer state $m_t$. This auxiliary variable tracks the maximum log-values encountered across time steps involving both input and forget gates. By subtracting this running maximum from the raw logarithmic values before applying the exponential function—a technique akin to log-sum-exp tricks—the network ensures that all intermediate calculations remain within safe floating-point ranges while preserving relative magnitudes. Furthermore, because exponential gating removes implicit bounds on how much information accumulates in $c_t$, a normalizer state $n_t$ is introduced alongside it. This normalizer acts as a dynamic denominator that sums up products of input gates and future forget gates over time. When computing the final hidden state output $\tilde{h}_t$, the cell state $c_t$ is divided by this normalizer $n_t$. This division effectively renormalizes the accumulated memory content at every step, ensuring that signals do not explode or vanish regardless of how many timesteps have passed or how aggressively gates have opened or closed since inception.

# Forward Pass Equations with Exponential Input and Forget Gates

To fully grasp the mechanics of sLSTM, one must examine its forward pass equations in detail, observing how each component interacts sequentially at time step $t$. The process begins with calculating candidate values derived from current inputs $\mathbf{x}_t$ and previous hidden states $\mathbf{h}_{t-1}$. Specifically, pre-activation values $\tilde{z}_t$, $\tilde{i}_t$, $\tilde{f}_t$, and $\tilde{o}_t$ are computed via linear projections involving weight vectors $\mathbf{w}$ and recurrent weights $r$, along with bias terms $b$. The cell input candidate is generated as $z_t = \varphi(\tilde{z}_t)$ where $\varphi$ is typically a hyperbolic tangent function ($\tanh$). The pivotal innovation appears in how these pre-activations are transformed into actual gate values. Instead of passing them through a sigmoid function $\sigma(x)$, which saturates near zero or one exponentially slowly as inputs move away from zero toward infinity or negative infinity respectively (though usually capped), we apply direct exponentiation: $i_t = \exp(\tilde{i}_t)$ for the input gate and potentially $f_t = \exp(\tilde{f}_t)$ for the forget gate (though sigmoid remains an option depending on configuration).

Once these raw exponential values are determined—or stabilized versions thereof—they drive two parallel recursive updates: one for memory accumulation ($c_t$) and one for normalization ($n_t$). The updated cell state follows an additive rule similar to classical LSTMs but driven by unbounded factors:
$$ c_t = f'_t c_{t-1} + i'_t z_t $$
Here $f'_t$ and $i'_t$ represent stabilized forms defined later in this section using $m_{t-1}$ and $m_t$. Simultaneously, a companion equation governs evolution of our normalizing factor:
$$ n_t = f'_t n_{t-1} + i'_t $$
Noticeably absent here is any multiplication by external candidates like $z_t$; instead simply adding scaled contributions allows us track cumulative weighting effects purely dictated by history dependent decision-making processes encoded within those specific gating pathways themselves rather than magnitude fluctuations arising solely from incoming signal strengths alone! After updating both internal registers simultaneously according above described logic respectively... we proceed towards deriving actual observable outputs visible externally via another layer called Output Gate Mechanism wherein yet again conventional wisdom prevails somewhat differently compared earlier stages mentioned previously inside paragraph preceding immediately before present sentence structure itself! That said let's return focus back onto main topic at hand namely defining precisely what constitutes stable transformation required ensure numerical safety throughout entire calculation pipeline # Chapter 4: Core Innovations III - The mLSTM Variant and Parallelism

The evolution of the Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) architecture has historically been constrained by the inherent limitations of its scalar memory cell. While the original LSTM design successfully mitigated the vanishing gradient problem through the constant error carousel, it forced all historical information to be compressed into a single scalar value per memory cell. This compression creates a bottleneck for storage capacity, particularly when dealing with rare tokens or complex associations that require distinguishing between many distinct pieces of information simultaneously. To address this fundamental constraint, the xLSTM framework introduces the matrix-memory variant, known as mLSTM. This innovation shifts the paradigm from scalar accumulation to matrix-based storage, effectively transforming the memory cell into a dynamic associative memory capable of holding and retrieving complex relationships between data points. By abandoning the traditional hidden-state recurrent connections that necessitate sequential processing, mLSTM achieves a unique architectural property: full parallelizability. This allows the model to leverage modern GPU hardware efficiently, offering a compelling alternative to Transformer-based attention mechanisms while retaining the linear computational complexity with respect to sequence length characteristic of recurrent models.

## Transitioning from Scalar to Matrix Memory ($\mathbf{C} \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$)

The foundational modification in mLSTM is the elevation of the internal state representation from a scalar quantity to a dense matrix. In the classic LSTM formulation, the cell state $c_t$ is a real-valued number (or a vector if multiple cells are concatenated), representing a compressed summary of past inputs updated via addition and multiplication by gate values. This scalar nature implies that every new piece of information must overwrite or blend with previous information in a way that risks catastrophic forgetting or interference, especially as the sequence grows longer. The mLSTM addresses this by defining the cell state $\mathbf{C}_t$ as a square matrix in $\mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$, where $d$ represents the dimensionality of the feature space. This structural change fundamentally alters how information is stored and accessed. Instead of maintaining a single aggregate value, the matrix $\mathbf{C}_t$ acts as a repository for pairwise interactions between input dimensions, allowing for a significantly richer representation of history.

This transition is not merely an expansion of dimensionality but a conceptual shift toward treating memory as an associative system. By utilizing a matrix structure, mLSTM can theoretically store up to $O(d)$ independent associations without immediate interference, provided they are orthogonal or sufficiently separable. The update rule for this matrix state replaces the simple scalar addition found in standard LSTMs with an operation that incorporates outer products of vectors derived from the current input. Consequently, the memory capacity is no longer limited by the need to compress diverse semantic features into a narrow channel; instead, it expands proportionally with the square of the hidden dimension. This increased capacity directly targets one of the primary failures identified in standard LSTMs: poor performance on rare token prediction tasks where limited storage prevents accurate retention of infrequent but critical contextual cues.

## Key-Value-Query Formulation Within an RNN Framework

To operationalize this matrix memory, mLSTM adopts terminology and mechanics familiar from Transformer architectures—specifically keys, values, and queries—but integrates them strictly within a recurrent neural network framework rather than relying on global attention maps over entire sequences. At any given time step $t$, the model projects its input $\mathbf{x}_t$ into three distinct vectors: a key vector $\mathbf{k}_t \in \mathbb{R}^d$, a value vector $\mathbf{v}_t \in \mathbb{R}^d$, and subsequently utilizes these to form associations within the memory matrix $\mathbf{C}_t$. The key vector serves as an index or identifier for specific information contained in that time step, while the value vector holds the actual content associated with that key. These projections are learned transformations defined by weight matrices $\mathbf{W}_k$, $\mathbf{W}_v$, and biases $\mathbf{b}_k$, $\mathbf{b}_v$. Notably, unlike standard Transformers where keys and values are computed for all positions simultaneously to compute attention scores globally, here they are generated locally at each step to update an evolving cumulative state.

The query vector $\mathbf{q}_{t+\tau}$ plays a critical role during retrieval at future time steps $t+\tau$. When new information arrives at time $t+\tau$, it is projected into a query vector via weights $\mathbf{W}_q$ and bias $\mathbf{b}_q$. This query acts as an active probe searching through accumulated memories stored in $\mathbf{C}_{t+\tau}$. The interaction between this query and the stored keys determines which parts of past history are relevant for generating the current hidden state. Crucially, this formulation places mLSTM within the theoretical realm of Bidirectional Associative Memories (BAMs), where pairs of vectors $(\mathbf{k}, \mathbf{v})$ are stored such that presenting one member allows for robust retrieval of its partner even under noisy conditions or partial matches. By embedding this key-value-query logic inside an RNN loop, mLSTM retains its autoregressive nature while gaining sophisticated retrieval capabilities previously exclusive to attention-based models.

## The Covariance Update Rule: Optimal Separability and Signal-to-Noise Ratio

The mechanism governing how new information enters and modifies the matrix memory in mLSTM is rooted in statistical learning theory known as covariance updates or Hebbian learning principles adapted for deep networks specifically termed "Fast Weight Programming." Rather than simply adding raw vectors or scalars to previous states, mLSTM employs an outer product update rule defined as $\mathbf{C}_t = f_t \cdot \mathbf{C}_{t-1} + i_t \cdot (\mathbf{v}_t \cdot \mathbf{k}_t^\top)$. Here, $f_t$ acts as a forget gate controlling decay (similar to standard LSTM), while $i_t$ functions as an input gate regulating learning rates for new associations formed by multiplying value vectors by transposed key vectors ($\mathbf{v}\_t \cdot \boldsymbol{\mathrm{k}}\_t^\top$). This specific structure ensures that each new association contributes rank-one updates to existing knowledge without requiring parameter sharing across time steps beyond what is necessary for projection weights themselves—a significant advantage regarding parameter efficiency compared to transformers whose parameters grow quadratically with sequence length if implemented naively via caching alone though technically constant per layer usually). 

From an optimization perspective rooted in works like Sejnowski (1977) and Dayan (1991), this covariance update rule offers optimality guarantees concerning signal separation when inputs have zero mean—which is ensured here through Layer Normalization prior to projection—and binary-like target representations often encountered in discrete token spaces mathematically speaking maximizing
